<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn6"><p>Only results with at least one proteins and one metabolite occurrence are shown. Obtained using the Omics Fusion platform.</p></div></div></section><p>In the leaves of salt-stressed adult purslane plants, the glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism pathway had 11 differentially expressed enzymes and three metabolites. The enzymes are glycerate dehydrogenase (EC:1.1.1.29), phosphoglycerate dehydrogenase (EC:1.1.1.95), phosphoserine aminotransferase (EC:2.6.1.52), glycine transaminase (EC:2.6.1.4), alanine-glyoxylate transaminase (EC:2.6.1.44), glycine dehydrogenase (EC:1.4.4.2), aminomethyltransferase (EC:2.1.2.10), dihydrolipoamide dehydrogenase (EC:1.8.1.4), threonine synthase (EC:4.2.3.1), homoserine dehydrogenase (EC:1.1.1.3), and aspartate kinase (EC:2.7.2.4). The metabolites are L-tryptophan (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00078" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00078</a>), tetrahydrofolate (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00101" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00101</a>), and betaine (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00719" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00719</a>) (<a href="#f6" class="usa-link">
<strong>Figure 6</strong>
</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10296175_fpls-14-1187803-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bde8/10296175/21aaf1eec663/fpls-14-1187803-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="730" width="779" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Enzymes (EC number) and metabolites (KEGG Compound number) from the glycine, serine, and threonine (00260) pathway differentially expressed in the leaf and/or roots of adult purslane plants grown for 12 days under different concentrations of NaCl (0.0 and 2.0 g of NaCl/100 g of the substrate). Metabolites differentially expressed are shown as red circles (in both organs), yellow circles (leaves only), brown circles (roots only), and metabolites non-differentially expressed are shown as white circles. Proteins (enzymes) non-differentially expressed are shown as white rectangles, and those differentially expressed are shown as blue (leaves only) and red (in both organs).</p></figcaption></figure><p>In other ways, in the roots of salt-stressed adult purslane plants, the glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism pathway had four differentially expressed enzymes and three metabolites. The enzymes are alanine-glyoxylate transaminase (EC:2.6.1.44), dihydrolipoamide dehydrogenase (EC:1.8.1.4), homoserine dehydrogenase (EC:1.1.1.3), and aspartate kinase (EC:2.7.2.4). The metabolites are L-tryptophan (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00078" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00078</a>), O-phospho-L-homoserine (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C01102" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C01102</a>), and betaine (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00719" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00719</a>) (<a href="#f6" class="usa-link">
<strong>Figure 6</strong>
</a>).</p>
<p>The enzymes (EC number) and metabolites (Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes compound number) differentially expressed in the amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism and glycolysis/gluconeogenesis pathways, the second most affected in the leaves due to salinity stress, with 13 occurrences of proteins and metabolites each, are presented in <a href="#SF5" class="usa-link">
<strong>Figure S5</strong>
</a>.</p></section><section id="s3_7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Scanning electron microscopy analysis</h3>
<p>In the work of <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al. (2022)</a>, white crystals surrounding and on the stomata in the leaves of young purslane plants under salinity stress were reported. The compositional map of those crystals showed that Na<sup>+</sup>, Cl<sup>−</sup>, and K<sup>+</sup> were their constituents, indicating that purslane has a mechanism of salt exclusion operating on the leaves. In the present study, the scanning electron microscopy (SEM) images showed the presence of such crystals in what seems to be the xylem (<a href="#f7" class="usa-link">
<strong>Figure 7A</strong>
</a>) and intercellular spaces near and around the stomata (<a href="#f7" class="usa-link">
<strong>Figure 7B</strong>
</a>). Again, the SEM images with detectors of EDS showed that the three above-pointed ions were the main constituents of those crystals (<a href="#f7" class="usa-link">
<strong>Figure 7</strong>
</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10296175_fpls-14-1187803-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bde8/10296175/b734257937e3/fpls-14-1187803-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="956" width="708" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Compositional map of the elements and image obtained by scanning electron microscope (SEM) with detectors of energy-dispersive spectroscopy (EDS) of areas in the leaf of a salt-stressed adult purslane plant. Formation of salt crystals in the leaf vein <strong>(A)</strong> and in the intercellular space <strong>(B)</strong>, near a closed map of the elements showing that the crystals are made of Na<sup>+</sup>, Cl<sup>−</sup>, and K<sup>+</sup>.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="s4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<p>In the present study, adult purslane plants experienced biomass reduction under salinity stress (<a href="#f1" class="usa-link">
<strong>Figure 1</strong>
</a>). Biomass reduction due to this abiotic stress has been reported before for this species (<a href="#B8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8">Borsai et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#B14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14">Giménez et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al., 2022</a>). In a previous study done by our group, the salt effects on biomass reduction in young purslane plants were mainly due to the osmotic effect, which results in stomatal closure and restricts the entry of CO<sub>2</sub> into the leaf mesophyll (<a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al., 2022</a>). When comparing the response of young and adult purslane plants to a very high level of salinity stress, 40–50 dS m<sup>−1</sup> (2.0 g of NaCl/100 g of the substrate), it is clear, based on the morphological characterization of the shoots, that the adult plants are less affected by this stress. Consequently, B1 purslane plants become more resistant to salinity stress as it matures. According to <a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">Kumar et al. (2021)</a>, a decrease in growth and biomass on water dropwort [Oenanthe javanica (Blume) DC] could be due to the adverse effects of salinity on cell division and elongation. In the case of adult purslane plants, reduction in the level of cell division and elongation could also be the reason for the shoots and roots biomass reduction observed in the present study; however, additional studies would be necessary to confirm that.</p>
<p>Adding NaCl to the substrate increased the exchangeable Na<sup>+</sup> contents by more than a hundred times compared to the control, with a consequent increase in the sum of bases, cation exchange capacity (CEC), and base saturation (BS) (<a href="#ST1" class="usa-link">
<strong>Table S1</strong>
</a>). With more Na<sup>+</sup> available in the saline treatments, the roots of the purslane plants were able to absorb it in greater quantity (<a href="#ST2" class="usa-link">
<strong>Table S2</strong>
</a>), confirming the premise that salt stress induces the concentration of Na<sup>+</sup> ions in the plant cells (<a href="#B8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8">Borsai et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#B14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14">Giménez et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">Kumar et al., 2021</a>). Much of the absorbed sodium remained in the roots, and a small part was translocated to the aerial part of the plants (<a href="#ST2" class="usa-link">
<strong>Table S2</strong>
</a>), indicating that plants can reduce the damage of salt stress to aboveground tissues by regulating ion transport (<a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Ran et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#B41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41">Ran et al., 2022</a>). As for Cl<sup>−</sup>, the roots absorbed only a small part, which translocated to the aerial part of the plants. This result reinforces the assertion that, under salt stress, Na<sup>+</sup> is the most harmful ion that limits plant growth and causes salt damage (<a href="#B31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31">Li et al., 2019</a>).</p>
<p>One of the main consequences of high Na<sup>+</sup> and Cl<sup>−</sup> concentrations is the negative effect on the absorption and use of several essential nutrients, causing a nutritional imbalance in the plants (Cruz et al., 2018). Nutrient absorption suffers little or no impact from salt stress in salt-tolerant plants, constituting one of the tolerance mechanisms (<a href="#B16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16">Gupta and Huang, 2014</a>; <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Ran et al., 2021</a>). Maintaining high K<sup>+</sup> concentration is one of the mechanisms underlying salt tolerance (Britto et al., 2010), and it is what happens in adult B1 purslane plants, which did not change the concentration of other mineral nutrients but experienced an increase in the K<sup>+</sup> levels in the roots and the shoots of plants stressed by salt. Increasing and maintaining the potassium concentration under saline stress is important, as K<sup>+</sup> is essential for reducing the cell osmotic potential and keeping the water balance (<a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">Kumar et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Ran et al., 2021</a>). For <a href="#B64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64">Yassin et al. (2019)</a> and <a href="#B41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41">Ran et al. (2022)</a>, low uptake of Na<sup>+</sup> and high uptake of K<sup>+</sup> mean salinity tolerance in higher plants.</p>
<p>The single-metabolomic analysis in the present study identified six pathways in the leaves of adult purslane plants that were affected by the salinity stress (<a href="#T1" class="usa-link">
<strong>Table 1</strong>
</a>). When comparing the results from adults and young purslane (<a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al., 2022</a>), considering only those pathways showing a raw p (the original p-value calculated from the enrichment analysis) similar or smaller than 0.05, just two of them appear as affected by the salinity stress in both studies; they are phenylpropanoid biosynthesis and glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism. In young purslane plants, 10 of the 46 metabolites expressed differentially in the phenylpropanoid biosynthesis pathway. That number was only four in the leaves of adult purslane, and only one metabolite expressed differentially in both cases, the cis-beta-D-glucosyl-2-hydroxycinnamate (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C05839" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C05839</a>). On the other hand, in the glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism pathway, six of the 33 metabolites are differentially expressed in the leaves of young plants and three in adult plants. Again, only one metabolite was expressed differentially in both cases: L-tryptophan (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00078" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00078</a>).</p>
<p>In the case of single-proteomics analysis, when considering the proteome profile of the leaves from adult purslane plants, the phenylpropanoid biosynthesis pathway is not among those most affected by the salinity stress; differently of what happened in the metabolome profiles of the leaves from young and adult plants. However, the glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism pathway was among the most affected ones in the leaves, having 11 occurrences of differentially expressed proteins (<a href="#T2" class="usa-link">
<strong>Table 2</strong>
</a>).</p>
<p>Last, the MOI analysis that integrated data from the metabolome and proteome profiles of the leaves from adult purslane plants showed that several pathways were impacted by the salinity stress (<a href="#T3" class="usa-link">
<strong>Table 3</strong>
</a>). For the sake of this discussion, only the three top ones—accordingly to the number of occurrences—will undergo further consideration, and they are glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism; amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism; and glycolysis/gluconeogenesis.</p>
<p>Considering the results from <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al. (2022)</a> and the present study, derived from SOA and MOI studies, it is clear that the glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism is directly involved in the response of purslane plants to salinity stress. That indicates that this pathway plays a role in the resistance to very high salinity stress observed in this halophyte plant species and should be the subject of additional future studies.</p>
<p>Several studies report finding that salinity has a significant impact on glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism. <a href="#B66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66">Zhang et al. (2017)</a> showed that this pathway was one of the seven amino acid metabolic ones significantly enriched in the adaption of tomato plants to salt stress. <a href="#B19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19">Jia et al. (2020)</a> applied proteomics and metabolomics integration approach to characterize the mechanisms underlying the tolerance of <em>Malus halliana</em> Koehne, an apple rootstock, to saline–alkali stress, finding out that this pathway is among the ones most affected by this stress in this species. Derakhshani et al. (2020), using a single-metabolomic analysis approach, reported that salinity significantly impacted this pathway when studying the response of two barley cultivars with contrasting salt tolerance. By comparing the metabolite profiles of three soybean cultivars, <a href="#B21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21">Jin et al. (2021)</a> indicated that this pathway and the tricarboxylic acid (TCA) cycle play a role in this plant species’ response to salt stress. <a href="#B38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38">Panda et al. (2021)</a> applied a non-targeted metabolomics approach to elucidate the salt tolerance mechanism in <em>Haloxylon salicornicum</em>, showing that the glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism was among those pathways playing significant roles in conferring salt tolerance in this xero-halophyte species that grows in saline and arid regions of the world. At last, <a href="#B60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60">Wen et al. (2021)</a> reported that the ectopic expression of an MYB30 gene from <em>Citrus sinensis</em> in Arabidopsis improves the tolerance to salt stress and drought stress and that this pathway was among those associated with differentially expressed genes between wild-type and the CsMYB30 transgenic plants, showing upregulation of the genes.</p>
<p>
<a href="#B12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12">Geng et al. (2019)</a> used a single-transcriptomics analysis approach to study sugar beet salt tolerance, reporting that glycine, serine, and threonine metabolism and amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism pathways are among those most significantly enriched in the salt-tolerant cultivar. Using physiological, proteomic, and metabolomic methods to study salt tolerance in pecans, <a href="#B20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20">Jiao et al. (2022)</a> showed that amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism was one of the pathways most significantly enriched in pecan plants under salinity stress. By integrating transcriptome and metabolome profiles of mature and plump quinoa seeds imbibed under salinity stress in the dark for 8.0 h, <a href="#B63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63">Yan et al. (2022)</a> showed that the amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism significantly enriched. In the present study, this pathway was enriched mainly in the leaves, where all enzymes differentially expressed were regulated positively—present in the leaves of stressed plants but not in the leaves of control plants. On the other hand, in the roots of stressed plants, only one of the four enzymes from the amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism pathway differentially expressed positively regulated under stress. Considering the results from <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al. (2022)</a>, our studies employing SOA and MOI strategies using transcriptomics, metabolomics, and proteomics data, it is clear that the amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism pathway is also among the most affected by salinity stress but only in adult plants and mainly in their leaves and not their roots. Leaves and roots are plant organs with distinct roles in the response to abiotic stresses, and the role of this pathway in the resistance to very high salinity stress observed in the leaves (but not the roots) of this halophyte plant species should be the subject of additional future studies.</p>
<p>The carbohydrate metabolic process comprises 13 essential pathways, including amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism and glycolysis/gluconeogenesis (<a href="#B5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5">Berg et al., 2010</a>). In addition, according to <a href="#B35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35">Nong et al. (2019)</a>, carbohydrate metabolism is a fundamental metabolic process in living organisms, being one of the most sensitive to salt stress in red dragon fruit, where, within the glycolysis/gluconeogenesis pathway, eight enzymes upregulated after short and continuous saline stress. In studies carried out by <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">Zhang et al. (2019)</a> regarding the mapping of metabolic pathways in sesame subjected to saline stress, the metabolites altered in response to saline stress were mainly involved in amino acid metabolism, oligosaccharide metabolism, citrate cycle (TCA cycle), glycolysis/gluconeogenesis, and urea cycle, suggesting that these metabolic pathways may play roles in the rapid adaptive response to saline stress.</p>
<p>Glucose accumulation suggests downregulation of glycolysis and/or upregulation of gluconeogenesis, which provide metabolic flux for downstream metabolite production to acquire salt tolerance (<a href="#B32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32">Llanes et al., 2016</a>). In addition to glucose, the accumulation of other carbohydrates and sugar alcohols, such as fructose, turanose, arabinose, xylitol, D-mannitol, and glycerol, showed significant in <em>Haloxylan salicornicum</em>, a xerohalophyte, under salinity, which suggests a role vital role of these metabolites in the regulation of stress-induced oxidative imbalance (<a href="#B38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38">Panda et al., 2021</a>). In the present study, beta-D-glucose (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00221" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00221</a>) positively regulated in the roots and negatively in the leaves of salt-stressed purslane plants, while alpha-D-glucose 6-phosphate (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00668" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00668</a>) negatively regulated in both tissues. At last, the other two metabolites differentially expressed in adult purslane plants under salt stress, beta-D-fructose 1,6-bisphosphate (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C05378" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C05378</a>) and phosphoenolpyruvate (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C00074" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C00074</a>), negatively regulated in the leaves and positively in the roots, respectively.</p>
<p>Osmoprotectants act against the damage caused to the plant’s cellular machinery in response to a stressful environment, protecting the plant (<a href="#B47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47">Sharma et al., 2014</a>). They are organic, highly soluble, low molecular weight, electrically neutral, and nontoxic compounds, usually classified into three main groups: a) betaine and associated molecules, b) sugars and polyols, and c) amino acids (<a href="#B44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44">Saibi et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#B68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68">Zulfiqar et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#B36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36">Omari Alzahrani, 2021</a>). Membrane integrity strengthening, enzymatic/antioxidant activity balancing, and water adjustments are physiological responses linked to osmoprotectants’ action in plants submitted to abiotic stresses, including water deficit and salinity (<a href="#B47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47">Sharma et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#B44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44">Saibi et al., 2020</a>). Different strategies exist to increase salt tolerance in distinct plant species; the exogenous application of osmoprotectants and enzyme engineering are two examples (<a href="#B36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36">Omari Alzahrani, 2021</a>). In the present study, betaine was regulated positively in the leaves of adult purslane plants under salinity stress and negatively in the roots. On the other hand, L-tryptophan did upregulate in the leaves and roots of adult plants and leaves of young ones (<a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al., 2022</a>).</p>
<p>The single-metabolomic analysis in the present study identified six pathways in the roots of adult purslane plants that were affected by the salinity stress (<a href="#T1" class="usa-link">
<strong>Table 1</strong>
</a>). <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al. (2022)</a> did not characterize the metabolomics profile of the roots of young purslane plants because they were too small to be processed, making it impossible to compare the metabolome profiles of the young and adult plants, as done in leaves. However, four pathways affected by the salinity stress in the roots of adult purslane plants also did so in the leaves of young plants. They are phenylpropanoid biosynthesis; phenylalanine, tyrosine, and tryptophan biosynthesis; tyrosine metabolism; and isoquinoline alkaloid biosynthesis. Four of the seven metabolites from phenylpropanoid biosynthesis, differentially expressed in the roots under salinity stress, were also differentially expressed in the leaves of adult purslane plants; they are cis-beta-D-glucosyl-2-hydroxycinnamate (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C05839" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C05839</a>), 4-hydroxycinnamyl aldehyde (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C05608" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C05608</a>), caffeic aldehyde (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C10945" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C10945</a>), and sinapoyl malate (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/C02887" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">C02887</a>); and those numbers are shorter than the 10 metabolites from this pathway differentially expressed in the leaves of young plants (<a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al., 2022</a>). Phenylpropanoids are derived from phenylalanine and tyrosine and are involved in plant defense, structural support, and survival. They are phenolics compounds, which are the largest group of secondary metabolites in plants, have antioxidative properties, and can scavenge free radicals, resulting in a reduction of cell membrane peroxidation, hence protecting plant cells from ill effects of oxidative stress (Deng and Lu, 2017; <a href="#B46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46">Santos-Sánchez et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#B48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48">Sharma et al., 2019</a>). At last, when considering the proteome profile from roots, there was no single pathway common when comparing the list of most affected ones in the metabolome and the proteome.</p></section><section id="s5"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusion</h2>
<p>After characterizing the morpho-physiological responses of young and adult purslane plants to salinity stress and applying a metabolomics and transcriptomics integrative approach to study the molecular response of young plants and a metabolomics and proteomics integrative approach to do so in adult plants, these are the main conclusions achieved:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li><p>a) B1 purslane plants become more resistant to very high levels of salinity stress as it matures.</p></li>
<li><p>b) In the case of adult purslane plants, the reduction in cell division and elongation could be the reason for biomass reduction. However, additional studies would be necessary to confirm the following.</p></li>
<li><p>c) The roots of the purslane plants were able to absorb Na<sup>+</sup> in great quantity, and much of the absorbed sodium remained in the roots, with a small part translocated to the shoots.</p></li>
<li><p>d) The salt crystal-like structures, previously reported on and around closed stomata on the leaves of young purslane plants (<a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Silva et al., 2022</a>), were also seen in salt-stressed adult purslane plants. As before, we showed that those structures are constituted mainly by Na<sup>+</sup>, Cl<sup>−</sup>, and K<sup>+</sup> found in what seems to be the phloem and in the intercellular space near the stomata. Once again, it indicates that this species has a mechanism of salt exclusion operating on the leaves, which has its role in salt tolerance.</p></li>
<li><p>e) The MOI approach applied reviewed one pathway from the amino acid metabolism [glycine, serine, and threonine (00260)] and two from the carbohydrate metabolism [amino sugar and nucleotide sugar (00520) and glycolysis/gluconeogenesis (00010)] were the most significantly enriched pathways when considering the total number of occurrences in the leaves and roots of adult plants.</p></li>
<li><p>f) When considering the enzymes and metabolites expressed differentially under salinity stress within those three pathways, it shows that purslane plants employ the adaptive mechanism of osmoprotection, using various groups of low molecular weight compounds, collectively known as osmoprotectants, to mitigate the negative effect of very high levels of salinity stress and that this mechanism is prevalent in the leaves.</p></li>
</ul></section><section id="s6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Data availability statement</h2>
<p>The datasets presented in this study will be made public upon publication in online repositories: a) Metabolomics Workbench with study ID ST002537; and b) The mass spectrometry proteomics data have been deposited to the ProteomeXchange Consortium via the PRIDE (<a href="#B40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40">Perez-Riverol et al., 2022</a>) partner repository with the dataset identifier PXD041627 and 10.6019/PXD041627, and PXD041628 and 10.6019/PXD041628.</p></section><section id="s7"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Author contributions</h2>

<p>The Supplementary Material for this article can be found online at: <a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fpls.2023.1187803/full#supplementary-material" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fpls.2023.1187803/full#supplementary-material</a>

<section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="SF1"><div class="caption p">

Leaves <strong>(A–D)</strong> and roots <strong>(E–H)</strong>. Polar fraction, positive mode <strong>(A, E)</strong>; Polar fraction, negative mode <strong>(B, F)</strong>; Lipidic fraction, positive mode <strong>(C, G)</strong>; and Lipidic fraction, negative mode <strong>(D, H)</strong>.</p>

<span>Supplementary Figure 2</span><p>Summary of the proteomics analysis performed on representative samples from adult purslane plants grown for 12 days under different concentrations of NaCl (0.0 and 2.0 g of NaCl/100 g of the substrate). In the leaves, control and stressed conditions shared 103 protein identifications; 33 and 256 proteins were uniquely detected in the control and stressed samples, respectively <strong>(A)</strong>. In the roots, control and stressed conditions shared 397 protein identifications; 38 and 28 proteins were uniquely detected in the control and stressed samples, respectively <strong>(B)</strong>.</p>

<span>Supplementary Figure 1</span><p>Scores plots of representative samples from adult purslane plants grown for 12 days under different concentrations of NaCl (0.0 and 2.0 g of NaCl/100 g of the substrate). Leaves <strong>(A–D)</strong> and roots <strong>(E–H)</strong>. Polar fraction, positive mode <strong>(A, E)</strong>; Polar fraction, negative mode <strong>(B, F)</strong>; Lipidic fraction, positive mode <strong>(C, G)</strong>; and Lipidic fraction, negative mode <strong>(D, H)</strong>.</p>

</div></div></section><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material2_reqid_"><div class="caption p">
<span>Supplementary Figure 2</span><p>Summary of the proteomics analysis performed on representative samples from adult purslane plants grown for 12 days under different concentrations of NaCl (0.0 and 2.0 g of NaCl/100 g of the substrate). In the leaves, control and stressed conditions shared 103 protein identifications; 33 and 256 proteins were uniquely detected in the control and stressed samples, respectively <strong>(A)</strong>. In the roots, control and stressed conditions shared 397 protein identifications; 38 and 28 proteins were uniquely detected in the control and stressed samples, respectively <strong>(B)</strong>.</p>

<span>Supplementary Figure 3</span><p>Gene Ontology (GO) annotation classification statistics graph from proteins in the leaves of adult purslane plants under high salinity stress. Classified accordingly to biological process (BP), cellular component (CC), and molecular function (MF). Numbers represent the amount of positive hits. Proteins present in both treatments (control and stressed) <strong>(A)</strong>, only in the control plants <strong>(B)</strong>, and only in the stressed plants <strong>(C)</strong>.</p>

<span>Supplementary Figure 4</span><p>Gene Ontology (GO) annotation classification statistics graph from proteins in the roots of adult purslane plants under high salinity stress. Classified accordingly to biological process (BP), cellular component (CC), and molecular function (MF). Numbers represent the amount of positive hits. Proteins present in both treatments (control and stressed) <strong>(A)</strong>, only in the control plants <strong>(B)</strong>, and only in the stressed plants <strong>(C)</strong>.</p>
</div>
<div class="media p"><div class="caption">
<a href="/articles/instance/10296175/bin/DataSheet_1.zip" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" class="usa-link">Click here for additional data file.</a><sup> (4.2MB, zip) </sup>
</div></div></section><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material5_reqid_"><div class="caption p">
<span>Supplementary Figure 5</span><p>Enzymes (EC number) and metabolites (Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes Compound number) from the Amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism (00520) and Glycolysis/Gluconeogenesis (00010) pathways differentially expressed in the leaf and/or roots of adult purslane plants grown for 12 days under different concentrations of NaCl (0.0 and 2.0 g of NaCl/100 g of the substrate). Metabolites differentially expressed are shown as red circles (in both organs), yellow circles (leaves only), brown circles (roots only), and metabolites non-differentially expressed are shown as white circles. Proteins (enzymes) non-differentially expressed are shown as white rectangles, and those differentially expressed are shown as blue (leaves only), brown (roots only), and red (in both organs).</p>
</div>

</div></div></section><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material8_reqid_"><div class="caption p">
<span>Supplementary Table 3</span><p>List of differentially expressed peaks (mass to charge ratio—m/z) resulted from the pathway analysis module of MetaboAnalyst 5.0, by organ—leaf or roots. Data set showing the m.z, the matched compound (Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes id), matched form, mass difference, metabolite name, and Log<sub>2</sub> (FC).</p>

<p>The datasets presented in this study will be made public upon publication in online repositories: a) Metabolomics Workbench with study ID ST002537; and b) The mass spectrometry proteomics data have been deposited to the ProteomeXchange Consortium via the PRIDE (<a href="#B40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40">Perez-Riverol et al., 2022</a>) partner repository with the dataset identifier PXD041627 and 10.6019/PXD041627, and PXD041628 and 10.6019/PXD041628.</p></section></section></section><footer class="p courtesy-note font-secondary font-sm text-center"><hr class="headless">
